Example 2.25

BCFW cells from recipes

In this section, we review the conventions for labeling general BCFW cells from [1, Section 6]. Each general BCFW cell may be specified by a list of operations from Definition 2.13. The class of general BCFW cells includes the standard BCFW cells, but is additionally closed under the operations of cyclic shift, reflection, and inserting a zero column anywhere (cf. Definition 2.13) at any stage of the recursive generation. Since any sequence of these operations can be expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{pre}\,}}_I$$\end{document}preI followed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{cyc}\,}}^r$$\end{document}cycr followed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{refl}\,}}^s$$\end{document}refls for some I, r, s, we can specify in a concise form which ones take place after each BCFW product. We will record the generation of a BCFW cell using the formalism of recipe in Definition 2.26.

Definition 2.26

(General BCFW cell from a recipe) A step-tuple on a finite index set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\subset \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}N⊂N is a 4-tuple\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}((a_i, b_i, c_i, d_i, n_i),{{\,\textrm{pre}\,}}_{I_i}, {{\,\textrm{cyc}\,}}^{r_i}, {{\,\textrm{refl}\,}}^{s_i}),\end{aligned}$$\end{document}((ai,bi,ci,di,ni),preIi,cycri,reflsi),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_i \subseteq N$$\end{document}Ii⊆N such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_i$$\end{document}ni is the largest element in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N {\setminus } I_i$$\end{document}N\Ii, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_i<b_i$$\end{document}ai<bi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_i<d_i<n_i$$\end{document}ci<di<ni are both consecutive in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\setminus I_i$$\end{document}N\Ii, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le r_i < |N|$$\end{document}0≤ri<|N|, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_i \in \{0,1\}$$\end{document}si∈{0,1}. A step-tuple records in order: a BCFW product of two cells using indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_i, b_i, c_i, d_i, n_i)$$\end{document}(ai,bi,ci,di,ni); zero column insertions in positions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_i$$\end{document}Ii; applying the cyclic shift \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_i$$\end{document}ri times; and applying reflection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_i$$\end{document}si times. Note that some of these operations may be the identity. Each operation in a step-tuple which is not the identity is called a step.

A recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}$$\end{document}r on N is either the empty set (the trivial recipe on N, denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_L$$\end{document}NL followed by a recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}_R$$\end{document}rR on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_R$$\end{document}NR followed by a step-tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((a_k, b_k, c_k, d_k, n_k),{{\,\textrm{pre}\,}}_{I_k}, {{\,\textrm{cyc}\,}}^{r_k}, {{\,\textrm{refl}\,}}^{s_k})$$\end{document}((ak,bk,ck,dk,nk),preIk,cycrk,reflsk) on N, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}$$\end{document}r consists of k step-tuples, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{\mathfrak {r}} \subset {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k, N}^{\scriptscriptstyle \ge 0}$$\end{document}Sr⊂Grk,N≥0.

Remark 2.28

(Recipe from a chord diagram) We now explain how a chord diagram D gives rise to a recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}(D)$$\end{document}r(D). Let D be a chord diagram with k chords on a set of markers N. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=0$$\end{document}k=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}(D)$$\end{document}r(D) is the trivial recipe on N. Otherwise, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_k, b_k, c_k, d_k)$$\end{document}(ak,bk,ck,dk) denote the rightmost top chord, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n:=\max N$$\end{document}n:=maxN, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_k:= \{p\in N\ \vert \ d_k<p<n\}.$$\end{document}Ik:={p∈N|dk<p<n}. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{D}$$\end{document}D¯ be the chord diagram obtained from D by removing the markers in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_k$$\end{document}Ik, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_L$$\end{document}DL and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_R$$\end{document}DR be the left and right subdiagrams of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{D}$$\end{document}D¯, on marker sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_L\subseteq N{\setminus } I_k$$\end{document}NL⊆N\Ik and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_R\subseteq N{\setminus } I_k$$\end{document}NR⊆N\Ik, respectively. Then, the recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}(D)$$\end{document}r(D) from D is recursively constructed as the recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}(D_L)$$\end{document}r(DL) followed by the recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}(D_R)$$\end{document}r(DR) followed by the step-tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$((a_k, b_k, c_k, d_k, n), {{\,\textrm{pre}\,}}_{I_k})$$\end{document}((ak,bk,ck,dk,n),preIk) on N.

Example 2.29

We now illustrate Remark 2.28 on the chord diagram \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_L$$\end{document}DL of Example 2.25, which is pictured in Fig. 5. In this case, we obtain the recipe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \mathfrak {r}^{{{\,\textrm{triv}\,}}}_{\{1,2,15\}},\mathfrak {r}^{{{\,\textrm{triv}\,}}}_{\{2,3,4,15\}}, \mathfrak {r}^{{{\,\textrm{triv}\,}}}_{\{4,5,6,15\}},((3,4,5,6,15)), \mathfrak {r}^{{{\,\textrm{triv}\,}}}_{\{6,7,8,9,15\}},((5,6,8,9,15)),\\ & \quad ((1,2,8,9,15)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}r{1,2,15}triv,r{2,3,4,15}triv,r{4,5,6,15}triv,((3,4,5,6,15)),r{6,7,8,9,15}triv,((5,6,8,9,15)),((1,2,8,9,15)).

Because our arguments are frequently recursive, we need some notation for the BCFW cells obtained by deleting the final step of a recipe. We use the following notation throughout.

Notation 2.30

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}$$\end{document}r be a recipe for a BCFW cell \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k, N}^{\scriptscriptstyle \ge 0}$$\end{document}S∈Grk,N≥0. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {FStep}$$\end{document}FStep denote the final step, which is either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_k,b_k,c_k,d_k,n_k), {{\,\textrm{pre}\,}}_{I_k}, {{\,\textrm{cyc}\,}}$$\end{document}(ak,bk,ck,dk,nk),preIk,cyc or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{refl}\,}}$$\end{document}refl. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {FStep}\ne (a_k, b_k, c_k, d_k, n_k)$$\end{document}FStep≠(ak,bk,ck,dk,nk), then we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}$$\end{document}p denote the recipe obtained by replacing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {FStep}$$\end{document}FStep with the identity. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{\mathfrak {p}}$$\end{document}Sp is again a BCFW cell. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {FStep}=(a_k, b_k, c_k, d_k, n_k)$$\end{document}FStep=(ak,bk,ck,dk,nk), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}_L$$\end{document}rL and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}_R$$\end{document}rR denote the recipes on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_L$$\end{document}NL and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_R$$\end{document}NR as in Definition 2.26. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}_L, \mathfrak {r}_R$$\end{document}rL,rR are recipes for BCFW cells \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_L \subset {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k_L, N_L}^{\scriptscriptstyle \ge 0}$$\end{document}SL⊂GrkL,NL≥0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_R\subset {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k_R, N_R}^{\scriptscriptstyle \ge 0}$$\end{document}SR⊂GrkR,NR≥0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S = S_L \bowtie S_R$$\end{document}S=SL⋈SR. Note that to avoid clutter, we will usually use L, R as subscripts rather than writing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{\mathfrak {r}_L}, S_{\mathfrak {r}_R}$$\end{document}SrL,SrR.

Remark 2.31

In contrast with the bijective correspondence between standard BCFW cells and chord diagrams, multiple recipes could give rise to the same general BCFW cell. Even the sets of 5 indices that are involved in the BCFW products are not uniquely determined by the resulting cell.

Background: cluster algebra and BCFW tiles

In this section, we review some of the connections between BCFW tiles and the cluster algebra of the Grassmannian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{4,n}$$\end{document}Gr4,n. See, e.g., [1, Section 3] for a relevant review on cluster algebras.

Product promotion

A key ingredient for connecting BCFW tiles to cluster algebras is product promotion—a map which is the algebraic counterpart of the BCFW product.Fig. 6Illustration of building up a BCFW cell using the recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}$$\end{document}r of Example 2.27. Box i shows the result after the first i step-tuples. The result of the step \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a_i,b_i,c_i,d_i,n_i)$$\end{document}(ai,bi,ci,di,ni) is shown on the left in each box, and the results of the steps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{ pre}_{I_i}, \text{ cyc}^{r_i}$$\end{document}preIi,cycri and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{ refl}^{s_i}$$\end{document}reflsi are shown on the right

Definition 3.1

Using Notation 2.10, product promotion is the homomorphism\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{B} = \Psi : \mathbb {C}(\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_L})\times \mathbb {C}(\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_R}) \rightarrow \mathbb {C}(\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,n}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΨB=Ψ:C(Gr^4,NL)×C(Gr^4,NR)→C(Gr^4,n),induced by the following substitution:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {on } \widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_L}: b \;\mapsto \; \frac{(ba)\cap (cdn)}{\langle a\,c\,d\,n \rangle }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}onGr^4,NL:b↦(ba)∩(cdn)⟨acdn⟩,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {on } \widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_R}: n \;\mapsto \; \frac{(ba)\cap (cdn)}{\langle a\,b\,c\,d \rangle } , \, d \;\mapsto \; \frac{(dc)\cap (abn)}{\langle a\,b\,c\,n \rangle }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}onGr^4,NR:n↦(ba)∩(cdn)⟨abcd⟩,d↦(dc)∩(abn)⟨abcn⟩.

The vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(ij)\cap (rsq):=v_i \langle j \, r \, s\, q \rangle -v_j \langle i \, r \, s\, q \rangle = - v_r \langle i \, j \, s\, q \rangle +v_s \langle i \, j \, r\, q \rangle -v_q \langle i \, j \, r\, s \rangle $$\end{document}(ij)∩(rsq):=vi⟨jrsq⟩-vj⟨irsq⟩=-vr⟨ijsq⟩+vs⟨ijrq⟩-vq⟨ijrs⟩ is in the intersection of the 2-plane and the 3-plane spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_i,v_j$$\end{document}vi,vj and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_r,v_s,v_q$$\end{document}vr,vs,vq, respectively.

Theorem 3.2 says7 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ is a quasi-homomorphism from the cluster algebra8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}[\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_L}^{\circ }] \times \mathbb {C}[\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_R}^{\circ }]$$\end{document}C[Gr^4,NL∘]×C[Gr^4,NR∘] to the cluster algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}[\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,n}^{\circ }]$$\end{document}C[Gr^4,n∘]. See [1, Definition 3.23] or [9, Definition 3.1, Proposition 3.2] for the definition of a quasi-homomorphism.

Theorem 3.2

[1, Theorem 4.7] Product promotion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ is a quasi-homomorphism of cluster algebras. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ maps a cluster variable (respectively, cluster) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}[\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_L}^{\circ }] \times \mathbb {C}[\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_R}^{\circ }]$$\end{document}C[Gr^4,NL∘]×C[Gr^4,NR∘], to a cluster variable (respectively, sub-cluster) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}[\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,n}^{\circ }]$$\end{document}C[Gr^4,n∘], up to multiplication by Laurent monomials in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T'}:=\{ \langle a\,b\,c\,n \rangle , \langle a\,b\,c\,d \rangle , \langle b\,c\,d\,n \rangle , \langle a\,c\,d\,n \rangle \}$$\end{document}T′:={⟨abcn⟩,⟨abcd⟩,⟨bcdn⟩,⟨acdn⟩}.

Remark 3.3

Definition 3.1 and Theorem 3.2 extend also to the degenerate cases, e.g., for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=1$$\end{document}a=1 (upper promotion), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi :\mathbb {C}(\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_R}) \rightarrow \mathbb {C}(\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,n})$$\end{document}Ψ:C(Gr^4,NR)→C(Gr^4,n), see [1, Section 4.3].

Definition 3.4

Let x be a cluster variable of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}[\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_L}^{\circ }]$$\end{document}C[Gr^4,NL∘] or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}[\widehat{{{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}}_{4,N_R}^{\circ }]$$\end{document}C[Gr^4,NR∘]. We define the rescaled product promotion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Psi }(x)$$\end{document}Ψ¯(x) of x to be the cluster variable of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{4,n}$$\end{document}Gr4,n obtained from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (x)$$\end{document}Ψ(x) by removing9 the Laurent monomial in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T'}$$\end{document}T′ (c.f. Theorem 3.2).

The fact that product promotion is a cluster quasi-homomorphism may be of independent interest in the study of the cluster structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{4,n}$$\end{document}Gr4,n. Much of the work thus far on the cluster structure of the Grassmannian has focused on cluster variables which are polynomials in Plücker coordinates with low degree; by contrast, the cluster variables we obtain can have arbitrarily high degree in Plücker coordinates. We introduce the following notation:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle a\,b\,c \,|\,d\,e \,|\,f\,g\,h \rangle := \langle a \, b \, c \, (d \, e) \cap (f \, g\, h) \rangle =\langle a\,b\,c\,d \rangle \,\langle e\,f\,g\,h \rangle - \langle a\,b\,c\,e \rangle \,\langle d\,f\,g\,h \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨abc|de|fgh⟩:=⟨abc(de)∩(fgh)⟩=⟨abcd⟩⟨efgh⟩-⟨abce⟩⟨dfgh⟩.More generally, we consider polynomials called chain polynomials of degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s+1$$\end{document}s+1 as follows (see [1, Definition 2.5]):2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\langle a_0\,b_0\,c_0 \,|\,d_{1,0}\,d_{1,1} \,|\,b_1\,c_1 \,|\,d_{2,0}\,d_{2,1} \,|\,b_2\,c_2 \,|\,\dots \,|\,d_{s,0}\,d_{s,1} \,|\,b_s\,c_s\,a_s \rangle \nonumber \\&\quad = \sum _{t \in \{0,1\}^s}\;(-1)^{t_1+\dots +t_s} \,\langle a_0\,b_0\,c_0\,d_{1,{t_1}} \rangle \, \langle d_{1,{1-t_1}}\,b_1\,c_1\,d_{2,{t_2}} \rangle \, \langle d_{2,{1-t_2}}\,b_2\,c_2\,d_{3,{t_3}} \rangle \, \nonumber \\&\qquad \cdots \, \langle d_{s,{1-t_s}}\,b_s\,c_s\,a_s \rangle \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨a0b0c0|d1,0d1,1|b1c1|d2,0d2,1|b2c2|⋯|ds,0ds,1|bscsas⟩=∑t∈{0,1}s(-1)t1+⋯+ts⟨a0b0c0d1,t1⟩⟨d1,1-t1b1c1d2,t2⟩⟨d2,1-t2b2c2d3,t3⟩⋯⟨ds,1-tsbscsas⟩

Example 3.5

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_L$$\end{document}NL and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_R$$\end{document}NR as in Example 2.16, the only Plücker which changes is: \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Psi (\langle 1 \, 2 \, 4 \, 7 \rangle )= \langle 1 \, 2 \, 7 | 3 \, 4| 5\, 6\, 7 \rangle / \langle 3 \, 4\, 6\, 7 \rangle $$\end{document}Ψ(⟨1247⟩)=⟨127|34|567⟩/⟨3467⟩, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\Psi }(\langle 1 \, 2 \, 4 \, 7 \rangle )=\langle 1 \, 2 \, 7 | 3 \, 4| 5\, 6\, 7 \rangle $$\end{document}Ψ¯(⟨1247⟩)=⟨127|34|567⟩ which is a quadratic cluster variable in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{ Gr}_{4,7}$$\end{document}Gr4,7, e.g., obtained by mutating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{4,7}$$\end{document}Σ4,7 (see [1, Coordinate cluster variables

Using rescaled product promotion and Definition 2.3, we associate with each recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {r}}$$\end{document}r a collection of compatible cluster variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\textbf{x}}\,}}(\mathfrak {r})$$\end{document}x(r) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{4,n}$$\end{document}Gr4,n. This will allow us to describe each (open) tile as the subset of the Grassmannian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k,k+4}$$\end{document}Grk,k+4 where these cluster variables take on particular signs.

Notation 3.7

Given a cluster variable x in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{4,n}$$\end{document}Gr4,n, we will denote by x(Y) the functionary on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k,k+4}$$\end{document}Grk,k+4 obtained by identifying Plücker coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle I \rangle $$\end{document}⟨I⟩ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{4,n}$$\end{document}Gr4,n with twistor coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \!\langle I \rangle \!\rangle $$\end{document}⟨⟨I⟩⟩ in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k,k+4}$$\end{document}Grk,k+4 (cf. Definition 2.8).

Interpreting each cluster variable as a functionary, we describe each BCFW tile as the semialgebraic subset of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k,k+4}$$\end{document}Grk,k+4 where the coordinate cluster variables take on particular signs. This appears as Corollary 7.12 in [1]:

Theorem 3.8

(Sign description for general BCFW tiles) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{\mathfrak {r}}$$\end{document}Zr be a general BCFW tile. For each element x of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\mathrm{\textbf{x}}\,}}(\mathfrak {r})$$\end{document}x(r), the functionary x(Y) has a definite sign \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_x$$\end{document}sx on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{\mathfrak {r}}^\circ $$\end{document}Zr∘ and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}Z_{\mathfrak {r}}^\circ = \{Y \in {{\,\textrm{Gr}\,}}_{k,k+4}: s_x \, x(Y) >0 \text { for all } x \in {{\,\mathrm{\textbf{x}}\,}}(\mathfrak {r}) \}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Zr∘={Y∈Grk,k+4:sxx(Y)>0for allx∈x(r)}.

BCFW tiles

In [1, Section 7], we proved that BCFW cells give tiles of the amplituhedron \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_{n,k,4}(Z)$$\end{document}An,k,4(Z) by explaining how to invert the amplituhedron map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Z}$$\end{document}Z~ on the image \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{\mathfrak {r}}^\circ = \tilde{Z}(S_{\mathfrak {r}})$$\end{document}Zr∘=Z~(Sr) of each BCFW cell \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{\mathfrak {r}}$$\end{document}Sr. For each point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y \in Z_{\mathfrak {r}}^\circ $$\end{document}Y∈Zr∘, the preimage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} The coordinate functionaries are defined recursively in a similar way as in Definition 3.6 using product promotion. Moreover, they can be used to give a semialgebraic description of the tile. This is summarized in the theorem below, which appears as [1, Theorem 7.7].

Theorem 3.10

(General BCFW cells give tiles) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_\mathfrak {r}$$\end{document}Sr be a general BCFW cell with recipe \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {r}$$\end{document}r. Then for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z \in {{\,\textrm{Mat}\,}}_{n,k+4}^{>0}$$\end{document}Z∈Matn,k+4>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Z}$$\end{document}Z~ is injective on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_\mathfrak {r}$$\end{document}Sr and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{\mathfrak {r}}$$\end{document}Zr is a tile. In particular, given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y \in \tilde{Z}(S_\mathfrak {r})$$\end{document}Y∈Z~(Sr), the unique preimage of Y in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_\mathfrak {r}$$\end{document}Sr is given by (the rowspan of) of the twistor matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^{{{\,\textrm{tw}\,}}}_\mathfrak {r}(Y)$$\end{document}Mrtw(Y). See [1, Definition 2.19].

For a standard BCFW tile \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{D}$$\end{document}ZD, we call the coordinate cluster variables domino cluster variables or simply domino variables and denote them as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}(D)=\{\bar{\alpha }_i, \bar{\beta }_i, \bar{\gamma }_i, \bar{\delta }_i, \bar{\varepsilon }_i\ \vert \ 1 \le i \le k\}$$\end{document}x(D)={α¯i,β¯i,γ¯i,δ¯i,ε¯i|1≤i≤k}. See [1, Theorem 8.4] for explicit formulas for the domino variables. The formulas have different cases depending on whether certain chords are head-to-tail siblings, same-end parent and child, or sticky parent and child (cf. terminology in Definition 2.19).

Remark 3.14

One can show (see [1, Remark 8.2] ) that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_i$$\end{document}Di has a same-end sticky parent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_p$$\end{document}Dp, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\beta }_i=\bar{\alpha }_p$$\end{document}β¯i=α¯p.

Example 3.15

(Mutable and frozen domino variables) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_D$$\end{document}ZD be the tile with the chord diagram D from Fig. 3 and domino variables as in Example 3.12. Among those, the mutable variables are:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{\alpha }_5,\, \bar{\alpha }_6,\, \bar{\beta }_2,\, \bar{\beta }_4,\, \bar{\beta }_6,\, \bar{\delta }_3,\, \bar{\delta }_5,\, \bar{\varepsilon }_3,\, \bar{\varepsilon }_5 \;\in \; {{\,\textrm{Mut}\,}}(Z_D). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α¯5,α¯6,β¯2,β¯4,β¯6,δ¯3,δ¯5,ε¯3,ε¯5∈Mut(ZD).Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Froz}\,}}(Z_D)$$\end{document}Froz(ZD) consists of the remaining 21 domino variables. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_5 = \bar{\beta }_4$$\end{document}α¯5=β¯4 by Remark 3.14.

Definition 3.16

(The seed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _D$$\end{document}ΣD of a BCFW tile \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_D$$\end{document}ZD) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \in \mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{n,k}$$\end{document}D∈CDn,k be a chord diagram, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{D}$$\end{document}ZD the corresponding BCFW tile. We define a seed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _D=(\textbf{x}(D), Q_D)$$\end{document}ΣD=(x(D),QD) as follows. The extended cluster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}(D)$$\end{document}x(D) has the sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Mut}\,}}(Z_D)$$\end{document}Mut(ZD) of mutable cluster variables and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Froz}\,}}(D)$$\end{document}Froz(D) of frozen variables (recall Definition 3.13). To obtain the quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_D$$\end{document}QD, we consider each chord \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_i$$\end{document}Di in turn and check if it satisfies any of the conditions in the table below, and if so, we draw the corresponding arrows.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_i$$\end{document}Di has sticky same-end child \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_j$$\end{document}Dj, then the dotted arrow from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_i$$\end{document}α¯i to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\varepsilon }_i$$\end{document}ε¯i appears, along with the usual arrows of the “sticky” and “same-end” cases. In view of Remark 3.14, in this case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\alpha }_i$$\end{document}α¯i stands also for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\beta }_j$$\end{document}β¯j as they are equal.
